So that whensoever a man began his service, it was at an end the seventh year; though he had served but one year before the seventh came about; nay, if but a month, or a day before, it seemed sufficient; for in the seventh year releases were to be made of debts, and a little service would serve, when what remained was to be remitted? But then the law of six years' service here, and Deut. 15. 12. is to little purpose, unless the service begin so soon as the seventh year expired. To avoid this inconvenience, some say, and (it is probable) that the six years' service was not broken off but by the year of Jubilee, Levit. 25. v. 40. See Jer. 34. 9, 4.

**V. 3.** *{By himself}* Heb. *with his body*. Alone, or in his single person, and without a wife and children.

*{then his wife}* As he came, so he shall go, whether in a single or married estate; and it is like he had the privilege of a father to go forth with his children he brought with him, as well as of an husband, to go forth with his wife.

**V. 4.** *{the wife and her children shall be}* The wife shall remain servant to her master, that gave her to her husband for a wife: and the birth following the belly, the children (born in her service) shall remain (as the mother's) with the mother. But how could this separation of man and wife be allowed, since their cohabitation is so expressly prescribed, Gen. 2. 24.? Answer, first, the husband was not compelled to part with his wife, for he might have continued his service and society with her, if he would: secondly, this parting might be but for a season, and by mutual consent; thirdly, wherein it may seem to swerve from the ordinary rule, there might be a dispensation, or toleration of it for the hardness of men's hearts.

*{her master's}* Till the time of her servitude expired, which might be the seventh year or the fiftieth. This is to be understood of an heathen woman, for of such they were to buy their bondservants, not of the Hebrews. Levit. 25. 42, 44.

**V. 6.** *{Judges}* For perpetual servitude was a matter of too great moment, not to be made up by a private bargain; therefore (the public magistrate) must hear the case, betwixt the master and the servant; and be sure, that the servant freely consented to such a servitude.

*{door}* That is, the *door* where the judges sat, as some conceive; and then (when they have heard the case, and given sentence in it) the servant shall be brought to the door of his master's house; and that door is here rather meant; for the word is *deleth*, which is used of any particular door; not the word *Schahur*, which signifies *the gate of a city*; where judges sat, to hear causes, and to do justice.

*{bore his ear}* Either as a punishment for contempt of liberty, (which he might have had, but would not) or to be a token of his strict and punctual obedience to his master's commands, not to go over the threshold, or out of the door, without his leave; at least not against his will: or to note his ready obedience to hear and observe what his master commands; so some apply Psal. 40. 6. where the opening of the ear, (according to the Hebrew) may be rendered by *boring of the ear*: and so *obedience is better than sacrifice*, 1 Sam. 15. 22.

*{for ever}* That is, to the year of Jubilee. And at that time all Hebrew servants were to be set free, notwithstanding any engagement of servitude made before, Levit. 25. 40. Of the signification of the original word here rendered [*for ever*] see on Exod. 12. 14.

**V. 7.** *{sell}* As constrained by poverty, or with intent to prefer her from a servant, to be a wife, upon the promise of him that takes her; so to accept of her, either as a principal, or secondary wife: as (when polygamy was in practice) the first wife was usually chief, the other of an inferior condition.

*{as the men-servants}* That is, a woman shall not be dismissed with so little care as a man, who is strong and better able to preserve himself from injuries, and harms: nor shall an Hebrew servant be put off, as a stranger born of another nation; especially of the Canaanites, (who were to be slaves to the Israelites:) and by this law she must either be continued in the house, and entertained like a daughter, or a secondary wife, or if he part with her, after he has taken her to his bed, he shall procure an husband for her, by offering a meet price, and convenient apparel to make her the more acceptable, and graceful in his sight.

**V. 8.** *{If she please not}* Heb. *if she be evil in the eyes of*.

*{to a strange nation}* Because this was a general rule for all Hebrew servants; and the case here mentioned, requires more than ordinary charity. By a strange nation, some understand, one of another tribe, though an Hebrew, &c.

**V. 9.** *{after the manner}* That is, he shall give her dowry convenient for a virgin of her condition.

**V. 10.** *{him}* That is, for his son, or himself: for it may be taken of either.

**V. 11.** *{or duty of marriage}* By which somewhat is meant besides food and raiment, (for they are expressed) it may be that which is comprehended under due benevolence, 1 Cor. 7. 3.

*{these three}* If he remit or refuse to perform any of the three forementioned particulars, verse 10. then he shall let her go free, without paying any price for her redemption.

**V. 12.** *{he that smiteth}* Levit. 24. 17. Matth. 26. 52.

**V. 13.** *{lie not in wait}* But by imprudence, or negligence, or any casual mishap, has killed another.

*{God deliver}* Though a man be killed at unawares, yet it is God's providence it should be so: and he may be said to deliver him into his hand that slew him; because the Lord (who has the disposal of all men's lives, and before whom all are guilty of death) without man's purpose (by his divine disposal) brought it to pass.

*{whither he shall flee}* That is, to the cities of refuge, in the land of Canaan, Deut. 19. 5. Jos. 2. 2. Or, to the altar, whether in the desert, or any other place.

**V. 14.** *{from mine Altar}* The holiness of the place ought not to defend the murderer, he shall find no mercy at mine altar, who, without mercy, sacrificed his brother to his deadly malice, in a presumptuous manner.

Deut. 19. v. 11, 13, 19. 1 King. 2. 28, 31. See 2 King. 15.

**V. 15.** *{smiteth his father}* The blow (though it be not deadly to the receiver) shall be deadly to the giver; for he shall die for it: because his deeper obligation to all affection, and offices of reverence and benevolence to his parents, aggravates the guilt of any undutiful, or unkind miscarriage toward them.

**V. 16.** *{stealeth a man}* (Whether free, or a servant to another) shall be surely put to death; because, usually, they that stole men, meant to sell them for slaves: and so they did great injury to those, whose in right they were, and brought great misery upon the party sold; especially if he were free and ingenuous before; and this law is principally meant of stealing such a one. See on Deut. 24. 7.

*{found in his hand}* If not yet sold, or used like a slave, it seems hard, that the man-stealer should die, therefore some conceive, that if after the fact it could be proved, that he had been in the stealer's hand, he should die for it; yet the phrase and manner of expression, imports another sense, which is; that if the stolen man be not yet sold, and the theft apparent, (as taken in the stealer's hand) he shall die for it.

**V. 17.** *{curseth}* Or, *revileth*. Though the wicked words of the tongue break neither skin nor bone, they bewray such a malignity of the heart, (when they proceed from children to their parents) that God dooms such transgressors to death, and that worthily; for they requite evil for good, returning imprecations for prayers, cursing for blessing; and this with breach of the strongest bonds of natural obedience and affection, and the grossest degree of ungratitude that can be.

Levit. 20. 9. Prov. 20. 20. Matth. 15. 4. Mark 7. 10. See Deut. 21. 18.

**V. 18.** *{stone or fist}* With the hand, or what comes next to hand, as stones are commonly readiest for sudden violence, where the wrathful person would use some instrument of cruelty, which may do more hurt than a blow with the hand.

**V. 19.** *{upon his staff}* 2 Sam. 3. 29. Zach. 8. 4.

**V. 20.** *{punished}* Heb. *avenged*. As a murderer putting him to death.

**V. 21.** *{not to be punished}* i. By the civil magistrate. Though (before God) he be a murderer, yet because of the vile and contemptible condition of servants; especially of such nations, as by God's decree were to be destroyed, Deut. 7. 2. the master was exempt for giving an account of his act; at least from receiving of punishment, for any miscarriage in that cause.

*{for he is his money}* Because he gave his money for him; as David calls the water of the well of Bethlehem, their blood who adventured the shedding of their blood, and the loss of their lives to fetch it for him. 2 Sam. 23. 17. And so the poor man's millstone is called his life; because he gets his living by it, Deut. 24. 6. See Gen. 37. 27.

**V. 22.** *{mischief follow}* That is, either by death, or maim of the mother, or child.

**V. 24.** *{eye for eye, &c.}* The execution of this law belonged not to private persons, but only to the magistrate; which yet was not always to be observed with rigor; but these particulars are set down to show, that the punishment must be limited to a due proportion: as that an eye should not be required, in recompense of wrong in a tooth; or any member of more for that which is of less use or worth; and this must be taken, not by an arithmetical, but by a geometrical correspondence; for if he that had but one eye, had put out one of the eyes of him that had two, if the recompense were numerical, one for one, it were not equal; for then one man might lose a sense, the other but an eye, which is but the organ of seeing, without which he might see while the other remains; therefore the relation more agreeable to exact justice, (in such a case) was not an eye for an eye, but two for one, that is, blindness for blindness, but it was more charitable to make the amends by peculiar recompense, or some other way. Levit. 24. 20. Deut. 19. 21. Matth. 5. 38.

**V. 28.** *{the ox}* By this, if a beast must be punished, (and it is a law touching other beasts, as well as for an ox) much more should the murderer; since, though this law be concerning beasts, it is not given to the beasts, but to man; for to man it is said, *Thou shalt not kill*: and if he do, it must be done unto himself, as he did to another, Gen. 9. 6. yet the ox was to die, for admonition to man, to have murder in the more detestation; and to make everyone to look better to their beasts, that they become not the causes of death unto others. See on Lev. 20. 15.

**V. 29.** *{his owner also shall be put to death}* For a man not to hinder evil when he ought, and might, is to become guilty, as if himself had done the fact.

**V. 30.** *{sum of money}* Taxed upon him by the next of kin to the party slain, or by the judge, verse 22. See Numb. 35. 31.

**V. 32.** *{thirty shekels of silver}* Our Savior's taking upon him the form of a servant, Phil. 2. 7. and his being sold (by Judas) for thirty pieces of silver, Matth. 26. 15. is conceived by some, both ancient and latter divines, to be prefigured by this law.

**V. 33.** *{do not cover it}* This is to be understood of pits, in, or near some way of ordinary passage, for in the field, pits were to be kept open for cattle to drink at any time.

**V. 34.** *{make it good}* By this law, not only commission of evil is punishable, but the omission of care to prevent evil.

**V. 36.** *{Ox for ox}* Not an ox for a calf, or a sheep, but according to the proportion of the damage, and so the former verse is to be expounded.

# CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1.** *{Sheep}* The Hebrew word is *Seh*, which comprehends lesser sort of cattle, as sheep, and goats, by way of distinction from the greater sort, oxen, camels, &c. For though some kind only be mentioned, others are meant.

*{kill it, or sell it}* This aggravates the theft. See Annot. on v. 4.

*{five oxen for an ox}* Five with that which was stolen, and but four sheep, because the labor of the ox was to be valued, which while he was kept away, was a loss to the owner, perhaps greater than the price of the sheep: See Prov. 14. 4. The same proportion might be held betwixt other great and little cattle; as betwixt the horse and the goat.

**V. 3.** *{If the Sun be risen}* If he be stricken to death in the dark, the want of sufficient light, to judge who or what he was, and the want of means to bring him to just punishment, (for he could not be pursued in the dark) was some excuse; but if the sun were up, to give sufficient light, both to judge of the party, and to bring him to judgment, then to kill him, was murder; unless in the just and necessary defense of the wronged party.

**V. 4.** *{double}* Before it was fivefold for an ox, four for a sheep; here but double: the reason is, because if the thing stolen were found presently with the thief, the property not altered, the owner sustained less damage and trouble, and the thief showed himself not to be so artificial in his falsehood, nor so settled in it, as he that carried the stolen goods away, and either kills, or sells, or keeps them a longer time from him that has right unto them; but it is said, Prov. 6. 31. *That a thief, if he be found, he shall restore seven fold*: this may be meant of a thief breaking into an house in the night, who if he were taken in the act, and smitten to death, no blood was to be shed for him, verse 2. and having forfeited his life, he might pay dearer for his ransom, than the rate of ordinary restitution; and so he might pay not only sevenfold, but *all the substance of his house*, as it is in the same 31. verse: or there might be a larger restitution in Solomon's time, because there was no plenty, 1 King. 10. 27. Or by sevenfold, may be meant manifold, as Gen. 4. 15. Psal. 79. 12. Isa. 30. 26.

**V. 7.** *{double}* For money or stuff; whereas the restitution for cattle was four or fivefold; the reason whereof may be, because it was easier for the possessor to keep what was so in his custody, than his cattle in the field, for there he could not keep them under lock and key, as he might do the goods and money in his house.

**V. 8.** *{to see whether}* The judge must (by his prudence) endeavor the discovery of the deceit, if there be any; and the clearing of the accused party, if he be innocent: and to this purpose, he must use the ordinary means, whereby the truth may appear, as receiving of evidence from witnesses, and the ratification of that evidence, by oath. See verse 11.

*{he have put his hand}* That is, whether he have dealt falsely, either by denying he had it, or affirming he restored it; or pretending it was stolen from him; or by any other way of deceit, to defraud the owner of his right.

**V. 9.** *{he shall pay}* That is, the party who was put in trust, if he be found in fault, as fraudulently failing the trust reposed in him.

**V. 10.** *{no man seeing it}* But the party himself, there being no other proof, or witness; in such a case an oath must be the end of strife, as Heb. 6. 16.

**V. 11.** *{an oath of the Lord}* So called, 1 King. 2. 43. because when men take it, they take the Lord to be witness of the sincerity of their hearts, and verity of their lips; and implicitly invoke his vengeance upon themselves, if they swear falsely, therefore answering upon oath concerning a man's self is not unlawful. See Num. 5. 19. Matth. 26. 63.

*{between them both}* For the oath was to pass from the one, for the satisfaction of the other.

*{put his hand}* See Annot. on v. 8.

*{if it be stolen from him he shall make restitution}* If by any fault of his it were taken away, Gen. 31. 39.

**V. 15.** *{with it}* As sometimes the beast and his owner might be hired together: and if he were present, the hirer might have so much less care, as he would have the more of the safety of his own.

*{it came for his hire}* He that hired it shall be free by paying for the hire.

**V. 16.** *{If a man entice}* Deut. 22. 28.

**V. 17.** *{If her father utterly refuse}* For the children should not marry without the consent of parents.

*{according to the dowry of virgins}* That is, according to her dowry as a virgin, whom he has defiled; to wit, according to her state, and condition, that is, more to the daughter of a rich man, than of a poor, See 1 Sam. 8. 25. There seems to be one set rate for all sorts, Deut. 22. 29. But the case is not alike; for that in Deut. is when the maid was forced, or lustfully abused, without intimation of marriage, to induce unto the act; and then the father was for his part of the wrong in the abuse of his daughter, to have of the offender fifty shekels of silver, and he was to have her for his wife, and he might not put her away all the days of his life. But in this case (in Exod.) it is otherwise: for if her father refused to give her in marriage, he that abused her, was to pay so much, as might serve to marry her to another of a suitable condition: and if he were not able to make good that proportion, he was to be sold as in case of theft, v. 3. of this chapter.

**V. 18.** *{not suffer a witch}* Witchcraft is here forbidden, Deut. 18. 10. and that upon pain of death, 1 Sam. 28. 9. By witch, is here meant anyone that has any dealings with the devil, by any compact or confederacy whatsoever: the word in the original, is of the feminine gender, because the devil's craft most prevails with women's weakness, in that kind: but there are men witches as well as women, Levit. 20. 27. and their sin as heinous, and their punishment as grievous: the word likewise signifies one that changes anything before the sight, (as jugglers do) but if it be by sleight of hand, without diabolical art, it is not here condemned. Some have thought witches should not die, unless they had taken away the life of mankind; but they are mistaken, both for the act of the witch, and for the guilt: for first, the act of killing anyone in such a way, as is called witchcraft, is not the act of the witch, but of the devil; and sometimes it is rather his act to feign it, than his act to do it; for such is his craft, that where God has given him a commission to do hurt, or by his great knowledge (being a spirit, and of long experience) he does foresee in natural causes (though concealed from others) some imminent evil, he stirs up malice betwixt the witch, and the party, and offers himself (for his, or her satisfaction) to bring the evil upon him, wherein the witch has no part, but a wicked will; and in that the devil is more wicked than the witch, and would do what evil he can, though she would entreat him to the contrary: But why then must the witch be put to death? Answer. Because of the league and confederacy with the devil, which is high treason against God; because he is God's chiefest enemy, and therefore though no hurt ensue this contract at all, the witch deserves present, and certain death for the contract itself.

**V. 21.** *{Thou shalt neither vex a stranger &c.}* Lev. 19. 33.

**V. 22.** *{ye shall not afflict any widow}* Zach. 7. 10. Isa. 1. 17. Jam. 1. 27. Deut. 24. 17. v. 27. 19.

**V. 24.** *{your wives shall be widows}* The just plague of God upon oppressors, and proportionable, as well as just.

**V. 25.** *{upon him usury}* Which is a loan of money upon covenant for certain increase, whereby the lender intends, and endeavors to make sure both principal and use, though the borrower be like to lose either the one or both. Whereas in some cases the rich lender must be so charitable to the poor borrower, as to expect neither use nor principal, Luk. 6. 34, 35.

Lev. 25. 37. Deut. 23. 19. Psal. 15. 5.

**V. 26.** *{to pledge}* Deut. 24. 6. Job 24. 3, 9. Prov. 20. 16. Amos 2. 8.

*{by that the Sun goeth down}* If it be such a garment as without which he cannot comfortably rest in the night; and of such a garment the words seem to be meant by this timely restitution, and it is in effect, v. 27. forbidden the taking of such a thing to pawn; for it were a vain and frivolous thing to take a pawn in the morning, which without payment of money must be restored in the evening.

Deut. 24. 13. Prov. 22. 27.

**V. 28.** *{revile}* Nor speak any manner of evil of them, by reproach or calumny; nor wish any evil to them, by imprecations or curses Eccles. 10. 20. Acts 23. 5. Jude 8. See on Chap. 21. 17.

*{the Gods}* Or, *Judges*, v. 8, 9. Psal. 82. 1, 6. Joh. 10. 34. Magistrates they are so called, because they should (as God does) do impartial justice without respect of persons; protecting the godly, as being the ministers of God for their good, Rom. 13. 4. and punishing the wicked, who are malignant enemies to God and them.

**V. 29.** *{first of thy ripe fruits}* Heb. *thy fulnesse*. It is fit that God (who gives all) should be acknowledged as Lord of all, by an holy tribute unto him: and that that should be presented first, not only to profess his right, but to procure a blessing upon the rest. See Mal. 3. 10.

*{liquor}* Heb. *tear*. In the precedent precept, God requires a portion of dry things, here, of moist, for he is the author and giver of both.

*{firstborn}* See Chap. 13. 2, 12. & 34. 19.

**V. 30.** *{seven days}* Some conceive it must be kept seven days, that it might have a blessing from the Sabbath, before it were presented; but the certain reason is, because under eight days old it was too tender, and waterish, and not of so perfect a shape at first, as a few days after it would be.

*{on the eighth day}* It was not necessary to offer it on that day, for that was the first day it was offerable to God; but after that, it might remain until a fit opportunity to bring it to Jerusalem, and there to offer it on a feast day. See Lev. 22. 27.

**V. 31.** *{ye shall be holy}* See on Chap. 19. 6.

*{torn of beasts}* If the creature were either killed by a beast, or in part devoured by him, or killed by a man for sacrifice to God, or sustenance to man, and a beast came casually and tore any part of it, in such cases they were not to eat it: and by such a forbearance, they were admonished of decency, and cleanliness in their diet, and of estranging themselves from a participation with beasts in beastly qualities; especially in cruelty. Levit. 22. 8. Ezek. 44. 31.

# CHAPTER 23

**Verse 1.** *{Not raise}* Nor *receive*, as the original will also bear; the meaning is, you shall not endure the hearing of a false report, especially, if it be offered, to make you either a false witness, or a corrupt judge: for as the receiver of stolen goods is partner with the thief; so the willing hearer of a false report, is a partner with the slanderer: and if *a good name be better then great riches*, Prov. 22. 1. a slanderous tongue is worse than pilfering fingers.

**V. 2.** *{a multitude}* The greater number is usually the worse, and though (in respect of men) it may be more safe for many, than for a few to offend: yet in respect of God there is more danger to an offending *multitude*, than to a few; for he will rather bear with a few than many wicked ones, Gen. 6. 12. Chap. 19. 4. Some instead of multitude and name, read *great men* as opposed to *poor* in the next verse.

**V. 3.** *{a poor man in his cause}* Levit. 19. 15. In charity we must have respect to a poor man's necessity; but in point of justice, neither the power of the rich, nor the penury of the poor, but his right only, v. 6. must be regarded. See Psal. 72. 2.

**V. 4.** *{thine enemy's ox or his ass}* If we be bound to do good to our enemy's beast, much more to our enemy himself, Matth. 5. 44.

**V. 5.** *{if thou see the ass}* Deut. 22. 4.

*{under his burden}* If God command to help up an enemy's ass under his burden, will he suffer us to press down our brethren with heavy burdens, and lay oppressive impositions upon them? *Does God care for asses?* and is he careless what they bear who bear his image? See 1 Cor. 9. 9.

*{help with him}* Though they be at difference between themselves, they must both agree for a charitable aid to the oppressed creature.

**V. 6.** *{not wrest the judgment}* Nor strain nor strive against the evidence of truth, to condemn the poor in his just cause, or to acquit him in that which is unjust.

**V. 7.** *{Keep far from a false matter}* It is not enough to be no plotter, no promoter of a false report, or no agent in it; but we must show such a dislike of it, as to keep aloof from it, as if there were some danger of infection by coming near unto it. See Prov. 4. 14, 15.

**V. 8.** *{Take no gift}* Deut. 16. 19. Prov. 17. 8, 23.

*{blindeth the eyes of the wise}* (Heb. *the seeing*) For it raises the affections which (like a steam or mist about a candle) dims the light of the understanding, and conscience.

**V. 9.** *{the heart of a stranger}* Who when he is in a strange place, and destitute of friends, and (it may be) also of means, has grief enough of his own, (as you by experience know) and therefore you must not add oppression to his affliction, which (your own heart will tell you) you would not have done unto yourself. Chap. 22. 21.

**V. 10.** *{six years}* Lev. 25. 3.

**V. 11.** *{but the seventh year}* That year was the remission of debts, Deut. 15. 1, 9. and the manumission, or freedom of Hebrew servants; and at the beginning of the sabbatical year, (which was the seventh year) was the law to be read, in the feast of tabernacles, Deut. 31. 10. and the land was then to rest; This sabbatical year began not with the month *Nisan*, or March, (whence the sacred year was accounted) because so they should lose the harvest of two years: but it began with the month *Tizri*, that is, September, from whence the civil year (after the manner of the Egyptians and other nations) was accounted, so did they account their Jubilee in the seventh month, Levit. 25. 9.

*{let it rest}* Untilled, or unmanured, both for sowing, and reaping, which were forbidden that year, Lev. 24. 4. partly for sparing the land, that it might afterwards yield the better increase; but especially what the land yielded (of itself) might be for the exercise of the people's confidence in the divine providence, without their own pains; and for a charitable supply, first to the poor, and after them to the beasts of the field; for the providence of God makes provision for man and beast; yet the owner of the land or garden, might every day (which was not a festival) take such things, and so much as was necessary for himself and his servants, Levit. 25. 6. so that nothing were hoarded up, whereby the provision for the poor might be diminished. If any feared he should be a loser by such a cessation, or resting of the land, or doubted how he should be provided for, if the earth were not exercised for the yearly increase; God passed his word for a supply in the sixth year, Lev. 25. 20, 21. See 2 Kings 19. 29. 2 Chron. 36. 21. Jer. 25. 11. & 34. 11.

*{Olive yard}* Or, *Olive trees*.

**V. 12.** *{six days}* Chap. 20. 9. Deut. 5. 13. Luk. 13. 14.

**V. 13.** *{the names of other gods}* Not by way of liking, or approbation, for otherwise they were and might be named by way of historical report, and of reproof. Deut. 12. 3. Josh. 23. 7. Psal. 16. 4. Hos. 2. 17. Nah. 1. 14. Lev. 13. 5.

**V. 14.** *{three times}* Deut. 16. 16.

**V. 15.** *{Thou shalt keep the feast}* Chap. 13. 3. & 34. 18.

*{of unleavened bread}* That is, the Passover, or Easter, in remembrance that the angel passed over the dwellings of the Israelites, sparing them, when he slew the firstborn of the Egyptians, Deut. 16. 9. and that the Israelites went out in haste, not staying the leisure of leavening their bread. Chap. 13. 3, 6. & 34. 18.

*{in the time appointed}* That is, the time when (in Egypt, and in the land of Canaan) the barley harvest afforded full and ripe ears, for an oblation to the Lord.

*{none must come empty}* Coming to the tabernacle, or temple, (at the feast of Easter, Pentecost, and of tabernacles) none must come without an oblation; partly for sacrifices to God, and partly for provision for the Levites: and thus it was a duty laid down by law, yet for the particular oblation it was a freewill offering, Deut. 16. 10. wherein the offerer was free to make the proportion of his gift.

**V. 16.** *{the feast of harvest}* Which is Pentecost, or Whitsuntide; kept in remembrance, that the law was given fifty days after they departed from Egypt; but called the feast of harvest, because then was the wheat harvest, and the ripening of other fruits. Deut. 16. 13. Lev. 23. 17.

*{the feast of ingathering}* At the end of the year, that is, in September, when the fruits of the earth were generally reaped and gathered in: which shows that there was a double account of the year; the one for civil affairs, which was in the beginning of *Tizri*, or September: the other for sacred, (as for the festivals of the year) beginning with the month *Nisan*, See on Gen. 7. 11. containing part of March, and part of April, Exod. 12. at this time was the feast of tabernacles. Of this feast See Levit. 23. 34.

**V. 17.** *{all thy males}* From twenty years old to fifty; or as some conceive, to sixty years of age.

**V. 18.** *{sacrifice}* Or, *feast*.

**V. 19.** *{the first of the first fruits}* (Chap. 22. 29. 34. 26. Deut. 14. 22. Exo. 20. 30.) these were yearly separated from the rest of the year's increase, the quantity whereof (by the tradition of the Jews) was a part between the fortieth, fiftieth and the sixtieth part, at the discretion of the offerer.

*{in the mother's milk}* Either as affecting excessively the pleasing of the palate with too much curiosity, because usually flesh is boiled in water, and not in milk; or it may be the meat of a kid so young, that it is (in respect of the late yeaning of it) rather moist like milk; than any firm or solid flesh; and so not wholesome as that which is of more age; or this may have reference to the paschal sacrifice, (which was to be taken from the goats, as well as from the sheep, Exod. 12. 5. See Chap. 22. 30. Deut. 14. 21.

**V. 20.** *{an Angel}* Which some hold was a created angel, but this angel of the Lord, was the Lord of angels; for they are bound to worship him, Heb. 1. 6. Chap. 33. 2. Num. 20. 16. Acts 7. 38, 39. 1 Cor. 10. 9. Heb. 3. 9. & 12. 26.

**V. 21.** *{he will not pardon your transgressions}* 2 Kings 24. 4. If you persist in them, without repentance for them.

*{my Name is in him}* That is, he is of my nature, being my natural son, and with that nature he has my name *Jehovah*, Jer. 23. 6. See John 10. 38. 14. 11. & 17. 21. See also Deut. 28. 58.

**V. 22.** *{indeed}* Exod. 19. 5.

*{an enemy to thine enemies}* Gen. 12. 3. Num. 24. 9. Deut. 30. 7. Jer. 20. 20. This is the true holy league, made betwixt God and his people, which might make the wicked afraid to do them hurt, since though they be weak, God must needs be too strong for all his and their enemies.

**V. 24.** *{nor do after their works}* Levit. 18. 3. Deut. 21. 21.

*{quite break down their images}* Deut. 7. 25. God commands his, not only to forbear to worship idols, but to destroy them.

**V. 25.** *{thy bread and thy water}* Gen. 28. 20. That is, all things necessary for this present life. See Deut. 28. 5, 8.

*{I will take all sickness}* Chap. 15. 26. Deut. 7. 15.

**V. 26.** *{There shall none cast their young}* That was a great means of the multiplication of God's people, both in their persons, and in their herds and flocks, that God (by especial favor) preserving them from abortive births, which in mankind bring deadly peril to mother and child, or both: thus their conceptions were brought unto an happy birth, and their labor neither in vain, nor with such dangerous adventure, as accompanied the generation of other people. See Chap. 1. 7, 19. Deut. 7. 14.

**V. 27.** *{my fear before thee}* I will make them afraid at your coming, and send mine angel to destroy them; as Chap. 33. 2. Josh. 24. 12.

*{turn their backs}* God makes men courageous, or cowardly, as pleases himself, and so gives the success of the battle to what side pleases him.

**V. 28.** *{hornets}* Not only enemies armed with weapons, as hornets with stings, (as some conceive) but hornets, according to the Hebrew, such as were usually called by that name; for with such God might plague the Canaanites, as he did the Egyptians with the frogs and locusts; and thereby might destroy their enemies: the accomplishment of the commination we may read, Josh. 24. 12. See also Deut. 7. 20.

*{and the Hittite}* He names but three nations here, understanding by them the rest forementioned, verse 23.

**V. 31.** *{unto the sea of the Philistines}* The Midland Sea.

*{from the desert}* That is, of Arabia.

*{to the river}* That is, Euphrates. By these particulars he pointed at the extent of the promised land, several ways, which was bounded eastward, by the Red Sea; westward, by the Mediterranean or Midland Sea, southward by the desert of Paran; and northward by the river Euphrates: of this large extent of land (the Israelites reason of their iniquities) were not possessed, except for a short time, under David and Solomon. See on Gen. 15. 18. & on Josh. 1. 4.

**V. 32.** *{thou shalt make no covenant}* Chap. 34. 15. Deut. 7. 2.

**V. 33.** *{a snare}* Deut. 7. 16. Josh. 23. 13. Judg. 2. 3. By idolatry, to which the Canaanites were much addicted, the Israelites (if they lived among them) might be infected; therefore (for their own security from it) the idolaters must be driven out of the land. So far were they to be from having to do with them, in any near communion of cohabitation, much less of marriage. See 1 Sam. 18. 21. Psal. 106. 36.

# CHAPTER 24

**Verse 1.** *{Come up}* God called Moses up to him before, upon his coming down, he was to propose the words of his covenant unto the people, and to take their answer, and then (with Aaron, Nadab, and Abihu) to come up to God again.

*{seventy of the Elders}* Some choice men of those, whom Moses chose and made rulers over thousands, hundreds, fifties, and tens, Exod. 18. verse 25. who might be the same, who afterward were endowed with Moses his spirit to be assistants to him in government of the people, and they were to come to the tabernacle, and there to be as witnesses of what passed betwixt God and Moses: the number was proportionable to the service, and as some conceive, it was the rather chosen, with reference to the 70. souls which came with Jacob into Egypt.

**V. 2.** *{Moses alone}* He alone was to go up to the top of the mount, the rest might ascend a good way, but not so high as he, yet where were apparent signs of God's presence, verse 10, 17.

**V. 3.** *{And Moses came}* When he had received the law in Mount Sinai.

*{All the words}* Divine terror made them promise fair, but their own frailty could perform but little; nor is it a condition competent to corrupt nature, to be legally and exactly just according to the law of God, though they promise it never so sincerely.

**Verse 7.** Chap. 19. 8. Deut. 5. 27. See Rom. 8. 3.

**V. 4.** *{wrote all the words}* Not the words of the decalogue or law moral, but the other laws, especially the judicial. Such as are set down in the former chapters.

*{twelve pillars according to the twelve tribes}* Gen. 31. 45. Josh. 4. 8, 9, 20.

**V. 5.** *{young men}* That is, some of the firstborn, (who until the sons of Aaron were especially chosen for that service) were chief assistants to the father of the family, in offering sacrifice, and when their fathers were dead, succeeded in their office; whosoever offered the sacrifice, young men might be employed in preparation and assistance, in the service which required the strength of bodily labors about it; See on Chap. 19. 22. & Num. 3. 12.

**V. 6.** *{half of the blood}* The blood was divided betwixt the altar and the people, to note the mutual stipulation of God to his people, and his people to him, who were atoned and made one by the blood of Christ.

**V. 7.** *{the book of the Covenant}* See the first Annot. on verse 4.

*{and read it}* The book was not only read, but sprinkled with blood mingled with water, Heb. 9. 19. and it was so mingled, because otherwise by congealment it would have been too thick: besides, it would go the further, being to be sprinkled upon a great number who stood next in the room of all the people. See Heb. 9. 19.

**V. 8.** *{The blood}* Was sprinkled with reference to Christ, and it signified, that the covenant broken could not be satisfied without bloodshedding. See Heb. 9. 22. anciently covenants were made with blood, to betoken constancy in the covenant, even to the shedding of blood and loss of life. See Gen. 15. 9. & Heb. 9. 20. & 13. 20.

**V. 10.** *{And they saw the God}* As perfectly as their infirmity could behold his majesty, though in propriety of speech God is invisible, 1 Tim. 6. 16. because *he is a spirit*, Joh. 4. 24. Therefore as *no man has seen God*, Joh. 1. 18. & 1 Joh. 4. 12. and no man shall see him with carnal eyes. The seeing then here meant is, when somewhat is obvious to our sense, whereby we have cause to conceive, that God is present in an especial and extraordinary manner. See Acts 7. 38. Isa. 6. 1. Joh. 12. 41.

*{Sapphire stones}* The sapphire stone is of a clear sky color, Anselm. Boetius, *hist. lapid.* l. 2. cap. 42. which may signify the greatness of God's glory seeing what we admire over our heads, is below the glory of his feet.

**V. 11.** *{upon the Nobles}* That is, the elders, whose seniority, authority, and gracious endowments, made them noble and honorable.

*{he laid not his hand}* Or, *put not forth his hand to do them any hurt*, Gen. 37. 22. Psal. 138. 7. Though to have any vision or apparition of God's especial presence, has been taken for a presage of peril, a forerunner of sudden death. See Annot. on Gen. 16. 13. & 32. 30.

*{and did eat and drink}* Making a sacred banquet of the sacrifices offered, verse 5. and were neither killed nor cast down, but that they did cheerfully eat and drink as at other times.

**V. 12.** *{Tables of stone}* Signifying the hardness of our hearts, uncapable of impression, unless God write his law therein by his spirit, Jer. 31. 33. 2 Cor. 3. 3. Heb. 8. 10. & 10. 16. And withal they import the perpetuity of that law, because stone is most durable. See Chap. 31. 18. & 32. 16.

**V. 13.** *{and his Minister Joshua}* See Note on verse 18.

**V. 14.** *{Aaron and Hur}* See on Chap. 17. 12.

**V. 17.** *{like devouring fire}* Heb. 12. 29. and it might be not only like as fire, but fire indeed. See Joh. 1. 14. The Lord appears like devouring fire to carnal men, but to them that he enlightens by his spirit, he is like pleasant sapphire, verse 10. yet to neither did he appear in any similitude, for idolaters to presume to represent him in pictures. See Deut. 4. 15, 16.

**V. 18.** *{forty days and forty nights}* Deut. 9. 9. Of these forty days, the six days forementioned, ver. 16. are by some supposed to be a part of the forty, but it may be those six days were spent in preparation, and the forty after in conference betwixt God and Moses, ver. 13. It seems by ver. 13, 14. that Joshua went up with Moses into the mount, but how high, how long he stayed there, whether he heard what God said to Moses, or whether he fasted forty days as Moses did, Exod. 34. 28. Deut. 9. 9. there is nothing set down in the Scripture. See on Chap. 32. 17. some think he stayed at the foot of the hill, while Moses was on the top; some, that he ascended some degrees, but not to the top; some, that he was on the top, but not within the cloud as Moses was, and that he was sustained all the while with manna.

# CHAPTER 25

**Verse 2.** *{Spake}* After the moral and judicial law, he gives them the ceremonial laws, and that so punctually, that nothing should be left to man's invention: wherein some things have mystical significations of great moment, and may have allusions to holy things, yet to be too curious, as many are in finding out conformities to every particular, may occasion both vanity and presumption.

*{bring}* Heb. *take for me*.

*{of every one}* Chap. 39. 5.

*{willingly}* Judg. 5. 9. 1 Chr. 29. 9. Pro. 11. 25. Rom. 12. 8. 2 Cor. 9. 7. The chief grace of any gift is the goodwill of the heart, which advances two mites to more acceptance, than much more, either grudgingly given, or with vain ostentation, Luk. 21. 3. & 2 Cor. 8. 12. & Chap. 9. 7. there was another kind of oblation commanded Chap. 38. 26.

**V. 3.** *{Make of them}* For the building of the tabernacle.

**V. 4.** *{Scarlet}* A deep and costly red, called *Dibaphos* (in Greek) that is, *twice dyed*, once in the wool, and again in the cloth, to make it take a most perfect color, and the Hebrew calls twice dyed *the second worm*; for out of the scarlet grain there comes a worm, which ground into powder, and applied twice by the dyer's art, unto the wool or cloth, gives it a most perfect red color.

**V. 4.** *{fine linen}* Or, *silk*. But linen grows from the ground, silk comes from worms. See Annot. on Gen. 41. verse 42.

*{Goats hair}* To make a kind of sackcloth for the covering of the ark.

**V. 5.** *{Shittim wood}* Ver. 13. & 2. Chap. 26. 15. Which is thought to be a kind of cedar that will not rot, but Esa. 41. 19. it is called the Shittah tree, as a distinct tree from the cedar, yet shooting up in height, and growing in bulk to the stature of a cedar tree, having a leaf like a whitethorn, and haply agreeing with it in lasting and durableness; for it is commended for lightness, firmness, fairness, and for continuance, without putrefaction above all other timber, and though it grow not in the wilderness, it might border upon it, or be brought by the Israelites out of Egypt: whereof they might foresee manifold use, though not to this purpose. Some think they had it from Abel Shittim, Num. 33. 49.

**V. 7.** *{Ephod}* Of this See Annot. on Chap. 28. 4.

*{breastplate}* Of this see Annot. on Chap. 28. 15.

**V. 8.** *{Sanctuary}* A place for holy and solemn service to God, both to offer sacrifice, and to hear the law, to present prayers, and celebrate them, which at the first was the tabernacle, Levit. 26. 11. See on Chap. 26. 1.

*{dwell among them}* Not that God's essence or presence can be confined to any place, Psal. 139. 7, 8. but that he would there give especial evidence of his grace, and glory, and of his near relation to his people, in intercourse with them by his word and sacraments; that tabernacle was a type of the church, the mystical church of God's people, in whom he dwells as in living temples, 2 Cor. 6. 16. Heb. 3. 6. See 1 King. 6. 13. Rev. 21. 3.

**V. 9.** *{after the pattern}* God showed Moses a model, after which he was to make the tabernacle. See verse 40. 1 Chr. 28. 19.

**V. 10.** *{two cubits}* That is, of the common cubits, being in length from the bending of the arm to the end of the longest finger; some measure the cubit to a foot and an half, of our measure; and so the ark was in length, a yard and nine inches, and three quarters of a yard in height and breadth.

**V. 11.** *{crown of gold}* Crowns are commonly round, but this was a square erected border according to the figure of the ark. See Annot. on Chap. 30. 3.

**V. 12.** *{in the Ark the Testimony}* See 2 Kin. 11. 12. 1 Cor. 2. 1. When the ark was made, commandment was given, that the testimony should be put into it, Exo. 25. 26. By the testimony is meant the two tables of stone written with the finger of God, Exo. 31. 18. called the testimony, because by them was testified God's will concerning the people's duty. Before the ark, (wherein was the testimony) and near unto it, Aaron's rod was laid and kept, Num. 17. 10. And the book of Deuteronomy, was to be put in the side of the Ark, Deut. 31. 26. that is, by the side of the ark, for the preposition *Hbjin* [*besad*] there used, may be rendered by *a*, *ab*, *de*, *e*, and *ex*, which may note a distance from it, but withal a near situation to it. Some Hebrew writers say it was put into a case, or repository, placed close by it; for it is expressly said, *Nothing was in the Ark, save the two Tables of Stone which Moses put there at Horeb*, 1 Kin. 8. 9. and the same is repeated 2 Chr. 5. 10. On the contrary (in appearance, not indeed) the author to the Hebrews says, *After the second Vail was the Tabernacle, which is called the holiest of all*, Heb. 9. 3. *which had the Golden Censer, and the Ark of the Covenant overlaid round about with Gold, wherein was the Golden Pot that had Manna, and Aaron’s rod that budded; and the Tables of the Covenant*.

That which seems to be the difference (for the matter of it, as Whether any thing but the Tables of the Law onely were in the Ark, or Aarons Rod, and the Golden Pot of Manna also) is of no great moment in itself; yet some piously affected towards the honorable estimation of the law, think it advances the honor of it, that nothing should be thought worthy to be placed with it in the ark, and therefore they cleave close to the negative testimonies, forenoted out of 1 Kin. 8. 9. 2 Chr. 5. 10. On the other side, some conceive it an impeachment of the authority of epistle to the Hebrews, if the words thence cited, be not taken to be literally true, as they seem to carry the sense at the first sight; and Cajetane (besides the Servatians and Anabaptists, Sixt. Senens. *Biblioth. Sanct.* l. 7. p. 491.) took occasion to deny the authority of that epistle, because (as he thought) it contradicted the two testimonies forementioned, as some of his own side have observed of him, namely Bellar. *de Verbo Dei*, l. 1. c. 17. Jacob Gordonius on 1 King. 8. 9.

Hence commentators are divided, some resolving, that nothing was in the ark but the two tables of the law; some, that Aaron's rod, and the golden pot of manna, were there also. The reconciliation is made two ways. 1. By historical narration. 2. By grammatical construction.

By historical narration, distinguishing the times, thus: While the tabernacle stood, they were placed all in the ark; but afterward, when the temple was built, only the tables of the law were placed in the ark, (and therefore it was so punctually noted as a new thing in those two forecited testimonies) and the rod and pot of manna were kept in some other part of the temple; and they might be safer in the temple without the ark, than without it in the tabernacle; yet some are of opinion, that they were not in the ark, neither during the time of the tabernacle, nor in the days of Solomon, but afterwards in the time of Jeremy; when by reason of the Chaldeans, Jer. 49. the Jews, fearing the loss of such precious things, put them all together in the ark, and that ark into an hole or cave of the earth to be hid; and afterwards when the temple was repaired, the ark was dug up, and placed in it again, and those three things in it before mentioned: And as Adricom Delphus reports, when Jerusalem was taken by Titus, he had that, the ark, and pot, and rod, and the golden table, or showbread, and the golden candlestick, and other utensils of the temple, as a part of the spoils of that glorious fabric; Andrichom. in *Descrip. Jerus.* p. 15. num. 77. and some hold that Paul might have the relation from Gamaliel, or by a Jewish tradition, or by revelation from God, as he had the names of the chief enchanters of the Egyptians, Jannes, and Jambres, 2 Tim. 3. 8.

Against this, viz. the placing of those other two things, besides the law, in the ark, Estius on Heb. 9. 4. objects, that Aaron's rod was too long to be lodged in the ark, for that (says he) was but two cubits and an half long, Exod. 25. 10. and the rod was a long pastoral staff, such a one as Moses used when he kept the sheep of Jethro his father-in-law. But that is but his fancy, which has no ground; and two cubits and a foot, being above a yard and a quarter, might be long enough for the rod in respect of any use we find that was made of it; and for the negative, in 1 King. 8. 9. & 2 Chr. 5. 10. which says, *That there was nothing in the Ark but the Tables*: The answer, which some learned men give unto it, is, That there was nothing of that kind, viz. Nothing of the whole law in it, but the two tables, wherein the Ten Commandments were written.

This historical resolution reconciles the epistle to the Hebrews, to those other places, without varying a letter from the text, or from the sense, which at the first blench the words import. The other resolution is grammatical, and that is twofold: 1. That, whereas it is said *wherein*, or *in which*, the original may be rendered *with which*; for in that sense we find the proportion *ἐν* often used; As *there was a man with an unclean spirit*, in the Greek it is, *ἐν πνεύματι ἀκαθάρτῳ*, *in an unclean spirit*, Mat. 1. 23. *His word was with power*, Luk. 4. 32. in Greek *ἐν ἐξουσίᾳ*, *in power*; *The first Commandement with promise*, Eph. 5. 2. in Greek *ἐν ἐπαγγελίᾳ*, *in promise*; *That I may come unto you with joy*, Rom. 15. 32. in Greek *ἐν χαρᾷ*, *in joy*. Secondly, if we take the words *in which*, and *with which*, as these examples show, then the words *ἐν ᾗ* may be in construction referred not to the next antecedent, which is *κιβωτὸς*, *the ark*, though that be of the feminine gender; but to *σκηνὴ*, *the tabernacle*, which is feminine also; and so the sense is, *wherein*, or *in which* tabernacle was the manna, Aaron's rod, and the tables of the covenant (taking all three together) though the tables of the covenant were not only in *the tabernacle*, but also in *the ark*; and this construction has warrant not only by rule, whereof see Sixt. Amama, *Hebr. Gram.* l. 2. c. 2. p. 152. but also by many examples of the Scriptures; as Gen. 19. 2. 2 Sam. 21. 14. Psal. 99. 7. Mar. 2. 3. 2 Thess. 2. 9. In this variety of reconciliations, the judicious reader may make his choice; but none can be so simple as against so many probable resolutions, to impute contradiction to the fore-alleged Scriptures.

**V. 17.** *{Of the Mercy-seat}* This was the covering of the ark, made of pure gold; and not distinct from the ark but placed close unto it; and God sitting as it were, upon the wings of the cherubims, (which made the representation of a seat) on the ark below it. This narrow seat was a figure of Christ: the word signifies *a covering*: the meaning is, man's iniquity being covered by Christ's merits, God is merciful unto him, Psal. 32. 1. and therefore it is called the propitiatory, and Christ a propitiation for man's sins, 1 Joh. 2. 2. because of the favor and mercy communicated to men, through his merit: and this was placed betwixt the tables of the law, and the majesty of God appearing between the cherubims, ver. 22. to note that Christ stands between God and the rigor of the law, in our stead as our mediator, and that God might look upon the law through him, as fulfilled by him for our sakes, and on our behalf.

**V. 18.** *{Cherubims}* The word *Cherub*, as some Hebrews conceive, is compounded of *Che*, that is, *as it were*, and *Rabin*, that is, *a boy* or *a young man*. These cherubims were figures of appearing angels, or glorious representations in human shape, like beautiful youth, made with wings, to signify the expedition which is used by angels, in obeying the will of God; Hence the Papists would defend their idolatry against the second commandment: But they may as well take upon them, to root out whole nations by war, against the sixth commandment, and spoil men of their goods, against the eighth; because God gave an especial warrant to his people of Israel for them both against a general rule; when there is a general rule, as that prohibition concerning images, the exception must have especial warrant by the same authority that made the rule; but it is absurd from a special exception from a general rule, to set up a general contradiction to the rule; as the Papists do in this particular. See on Gen. 38. 3.

*{of beaten work}* Not melted and so cast in a mold, but beaten out of the whole piece with an hammer.

**V. 19.** *{Mercy-seat}* Or, *the matter of the Mercy-seat*.

**V. 20.** *{their faces shall look one to another}* Heb. *The face of a man turned to the face of his brother*; Which further confirms their portraiture to have been of an human shape: as is noted on verse 18. See 1 Kings 8. 7. Heb. 9.

**V. 22.** *{there will I meet with thee}* That is, above the cherubims and in the middle between them I will gloriously appear, and declare my mind unto my people, thence it is probable were formed sensible words. 1 Sam. 4. 4. Judg. 20. 27. 2 King. 19. 15. Psal. 80. 1. & 99. 1. Num. 7. 89.

**V. 23.** *{Table}* As it is described from ver. 23. to 28. See 1 King. 17. 48.

*{Shittim wood}* Chap. 37. 10. See Annot. on ver. 5.

**V. 25.** *{a border}* Made of gold bounding the table upon every side, and lying flat upon it like a guard upon a garment; upon this was set a crown four fingers high, yet not round, (as crowns usually are) but as the table foursquare; there is twice mention of a crown, ver. 24, 25. whence some take it, there were two crowns; but it is more like there was but one, though twice named.

**V. 29.** *{dishes}* To receive the fine flour, and other oblations brought unto the temple; and for the showbread, which was to be placed upon them.

*{spoons}* To put frankincense in, and to take it out of the dishes, when it was to be burned upon the altar of incense.

*{bowls}* For the drink offerings; into which they were put; and out of which they were poured in oblation to God.

*{to cover withal}* Or rather *to pour out withal*, for out of the bowls they were poured, and yet the bowls also might be covered.

**V. 30.** *{Shewbread}* Heb. *the bread of faces*; that is, the bread showed, presented, or set in view as before the face: the manner how, is set down, Lev. 24. 5. they were in number twelve, according to the number of the tribes of Israel; in measure each contained two homers; enough to serve two men for one day; and they were placed on a table, not in the holy of holies (where only the high priest came) but in the holy place; the place of the priests, not of the people; and six and six in a row, were these cakes or loaves placed, answering each other. This bread was (every Sabbath) set new on the table, and when the new came, the priests did eat the old in the holy place. The setting of these loaves was a memorial of God's beneficence to his people, preparing (for them) *a Table in the wildernesse*, Psal. 23. 5. & 78. 19. and to admonish them, that in taking their repast, they should have an especial eye to God, in whose presence that provision was set, to whom they were to give thanks, and eat to his glory, 1 Cor. 10. 31.

**V. 31.** *{candlestick}* Zach. 4. 2. Rev. 1. 4.

**V. 34.** *{In the candlestick}* That is, in the shaft of the candlestick.

**V. 37.** *{they shall light}* Or, *cause to ascend*: Chap. 30. 8. & 27. 20. 2 Chr. 13. 11. Lev. 24. 2, 3. 1 Sam. 3. 3.

*{over against it}* Heb. *the face of it*. That is, towards the table of the showbread: Num. 8. 2.

**V. 39.** *{talent}* The ordinary talent contained 54 pounds, 8 ounces; a quarter of troy weight; the sacred talent contained an hundred pound. So in some directions before Josephus: others augment it to an hundred and twenty-five pound weight, reckoning twelve ounces to the pound. See on Chap. 38. 25.

**V. 40.** *{pattern}* Chap. 28. 30. & 1 Chr. 28. 12, 19. Acts 7. 44. See Annot. on ver. 9.

*{which was shewed}* Heb. *which thou hast caused to see*.

# CHAPTER 26

## OF THE SANCTUARY, OR TABERNACLE, A BRIEF DESCRIPTION.

The sanctuary (at this time the tabernacle) was a movable house, made of boards of Shittim wood, whereof see ver. 15. and hung about with curtains, wrought with divers colors in a curious manner, and covered over with three coverings, one upon another, to keep it from harm. It was thirty cubits long, ten cubits high, ten broad: the uppermost part whereof was made slope, and somewhat bending, each way (as the roof of an house) to shoot off rain, that it might not rest upon it. It was divided into two parts, the one was called the holy of holies, at the upper end of it taking up ten cubits, wherein what was placed, see Chap. 25. 16. Into this holy of holies only the high priest came, and that but once a year, Lev. 16. The other part was called the holy place, containing in it twenty cubits. In this was the altar of incense, on which incense was burned; on the right side thereof was the candlestick, mentioned, ver. 31. and on the left the table of showbread, whereof ver. 35. These two parts were divided by a veil, and from the latter part of the tabernacle, there was a court of fifty cubits in length walled with boards, but without a covering above; in part of this court which was next unto the tabernacle, was the altar of the burnt offering, and the brazen sea, or laver for the washings; and so far might none approach but the priests, whose place was divided from the people's by a partition wall of boards, three cubits high and no more, that the people might see the priest at his sacrifice; and into this part, neither Gentiles, nor any unclean person might approach, Josephus Antiq. l. 3. c. 5.

**Verse 1.** *{The Tabernacle}* That is, a tent, or movable house for the ark, table, showbread, &c. See on Chap. 25. verse 8.

*{of cunning work}* Heb. *the work of a cunning workman*, or *embroiderer*. Silk or linen being the ground, threads of blue, purple, and scarlet, were curiously wrought upon it, to make it both various and glorious.

**V. 3.** *{five curtains}* So joined as to make up one great one; and the other five so joined, as to make up another great one, and these two after by loops, buckles, or hooks, so coupled together, as to make the tabernacle as one entire work.

**V. 7.** *{to be a covering}* Not only above, but round about; so they were as a case for the tabernacle (which was fine and precious) to preserve it both above, and on all sides, from the injury of weather; this covering was made of goats' hair, woven into cloth: and there was a third covering of rams' skins dyed red, ver. 14. and a fourth above that, next unto the weather (of badgers' skins) which are stronger, and coarser than the other; these curtains were joined together as the other, ver. 3.

**V. 9.** *{double the sixth curtain}* That is five on the one side, and five on the other, coupled together and, the sixth was to hang over the entrance of the tabernacle, the curtains being eleven in all, which number is made up of five and six, the sixth of the six, and the eleventh of the whole was to be doubled, and so to hang on both sides the door of the tabernacle. These were larger than the former curtains of cunning work, for they were 30 cubits long, ver. 8. the other but 28, ver. 2. because they were but a covering and safeguard of the finer curtains.

**V. 12.** *{remnant}* For these curtains were two cubits longer than the curtains of the tabernacle, ver. 2, 8. so that they were sider by a cubit on both sides, that they might the better cover the better covering which was under them, and save it from wearing or defiling.

**V. 15.** *{Shittim wood}* Whereof there was store in the desert of Arabia. See on Chap. 25. 5.

**V. 24.** *{coupled}* Like twins exactly answering one another: this is to be understood of the two corner boards in the back part of the tabernacle, mentioned ver. 23. which for more strength and firmness were to be double, above and below, and so closed together that the ring put in the corner might serve for the bars which met on both sides.

**V. 26.** *{bars}* These bars covered with gold, and put into golden rings were to strengthen and hold the boards together, that they might be firm and steadfast.

**V. 28.** *{midst of the boards}* Not through the thickness of them; but through the rings, which were fastened to the middle of the outside; the middle, in respect of the space above and below the bars.

**V. 30.** *{according to the fashion}* Acts 7. 44. Heb. 5. 8. See on Chap. 25. 9.

**V. 31.** *{vail}* Within this veil which separated betwixt the holy place, and the most holy, was the ark placed, ver. 33. 2 Chr. 3. 14. Matth. 27. 51. Heb. 9. 3.

*{with cherubims}* The portraiture of them wrought or woven into the work. See the Note on Chap. 25. 18.

**V. 32.** *{hooks}* Some read *heads of the pillars*.

**V. 33.** *{most holy}* Whereinto the high priest only entered once a year; here was the ark placed, and hence God gave answers; therefore it was called the oracle, in the 1 King. 6. 15, 19. & 6. 28. it was a type of heaven, the throne of God, for here God manifested his especial presence, and the high priest entered into it by especial prerogative; as Christ our high priest into heaven, and but once a year, the better to preserve the reverence of the place, for which cause it was separated by a veil from the rest of the tabernacle: See Chap. 34. 10. Levit. 16. 2, 16, 17. Heb. 9. 7.

**V. 35.** *{the Table without}* That is, the table of the showbread, or twelve cakes or loaves (representing the twelve tribes) shall be on the outside of the veil, not in the inside, (where the ark was placed) that is, it must be set not in the most holy place, nor on the inside in the holy of holies where the ark was placed.

**V. 36.** *{an hanging}* This hanging, or veil, was a partition betwixt the holy place of the priests, and place where the people were, it was not close to the ground, because at the entrance it was to let in some light. See on Levi. 4. 6. **ver. 37.** overlay them with gold. See on Chap. 36. 38.

# CHAPTER 27

**Verse 1.** *{Altar}* This was not the altar which served for oblation of incense morning and evening, but the altar of burnt offerings made three cubits high, and five cubits square, placed not in the tabernacle, but in the court, (which sometimes is comprehended in the name of the tabernacle) to the midst of its height (which had some Shittim wood about it) it was covered with brass, but within was, as some conceive, filled up halfway with earth or rough unhewn stones; or which is more probable by the description in the text, it was hollow unto the midst where the brazen grate was, upon which the daily sacrifice of a lamb morning and evening was offered, Exod. 29. 39. and other oblations of several kinds and occasions. It was first consecrated with holy oil, Num. 7. 1. and then other things set upon it were hallowed by it. Mat. 23. 19. This altar differed from such as were made of earth, upon extraordinary occasions, Exod. 20. 24. for this was to continue for the constant service of the tabernacle, which must not be so base and mean as the other, because the tabernacle was glorious.

**V. 2.** *{horns}* Made like horns sharp and crooked, partly for ornament, partly for use; and the use, as some conceive was, chiefly to bind the sacrifice to the altar, Psal. 118. 27. which was to be offered upon the altar.

*{with brass}* The wooden part of the altar, was to be overlaid or covered with brass to preserve it from fire, and yet it was strange, that the brass very much heated did not burn the wood; and there was, as some conceive, an especial providence in it, that it did not so, God as he sent fire down upon the altar overruling the operation of it at his pleasure, yet the altar being so broad, the fire might be kept in a grate at due distance from the wood.

**V. 3.** *{fire pans}* Which served to carry fire from the altar of the burnt offering, to kindle the incense, which burnt in chafing dishes upon the altar of incense.

**V. 4.** *{the grate}* Made like network, but with small holes like a sieve, was fastened by rings to the altar, and let down by chains to the midst of it; under it, as some conceive, was the wood and fire put, upon it the sacrifice was laid to be burned; others hold, that both fire and sacrifice were upon it.

*{four brasen rings}* The altar had rings and bars by itself, Chap. 38. 7. and so had this grate, that they might be removed and carried severally by themselves.

**V. 8.** *{as it was shewed}* Heb. *as he shewed*, Exod. 25. 40.

**V. 9.** *{the Court of the Tabernacle}* Which compassed the tabernacle on all sides: which was an uneven square, for it was an hundred cubits, or fifty yards long; and fifty cubits, or twenty-five yards broad, and five cubits, or two yards and an half high, which had for walls hangings of fine linen, borne up by pillars of brass. In this court and about the midway from the tabernacle to the outside of it, was placed the altar, in the open air, that the flame and fume of the sacrifice might have free vent without hurt or offense. Of this court fifty cubits from the east end towards the sanctuary, were for the sacrifices which were tendered by the people, in the part nearest to the entrance, and offered by the priests towards the furthest part of the fifty cubits and about ten cubits distance from the tabernacle; for so many cubits was it off the altar on the east side; and so many from the walls, or hangings of the court, on the west side, and 20 cubits on the north, and as many on the south side. In this spacious court there was receipt for all professions of religion, whether sincere or hypocritical, and so it was a type of the church visible in the world.

**V. 10.** *{fillets}* They were certain hoops or circles of silver plate, compassing the upper part of the pillar to beautify it, and it may be also to fasten the hangings in some places to the pillars.

**V. 13.** *{East side Eastward}* Or, *full East*.

*{fifty cubits}* Meaning curtains of fifty cubits.

**V. 16.** *{gate of the Court}* Which was on the east side.

**V. 18.** *{fifty}* Heb. *fifty by fifty*.

**V. 19.** *{pins}* Or, *stakes*. Wherewith the hangings were fastened to the ground, that they might not be waved to and fro with the wind.

**V. 20.** *{oil olive beaten}* Lev. 24. 2. Such as comes from the olive, when it is first pressed, and beaten, and rather beaten with a pestle, or mallet, than crushed with a heavy weight of a press, or millstones; because it should run clear and free from dregs.

*{to burn}* Heb. *to ascend up*.

*{always}* See on Chap. 30. ver. 8.

**V. 21.** *{of the Testimony}* That is, of the law (in the ark) the testimony of God's will, and of his covenant with his people, See on Chap. 25. 16. & 31. 18.

*{From evening to morning}* For all night, and not by day, were the lamps to burn; and though it be said always, ver. 20. that is to be understood only of the night season, as if it had been said every night; for they could not burn until they were lighted, and they were not lighted until evening, Chap. 30. 8. and from thence they were to burn until the morning, Lev. 24. 3. This might be a type or figure of that spiritual light which dispels the darkness and blindness of the mind, 2 Cor. 4. 6. 2 Pet. 1. 19. See 1 Sam. 3. 3. 2 Chr. 13. 11.

# CHAPTER 28

**Verse 2.** *{Holy garments}* Garments to be worn in holy ministrations in reference to Christ our high priest, who is *holy and undefiled*, Heb. 7. 26. and his robe which Christians must put on, to make them accepted with his father, is a most holy robe. See Isai. 52. 1. Rev. 19. 8.

*{for glory and for beauty}* Whereby his office may be known to be glorious, and excellent as representing him, who was, and is *the brightness of the divine glory*, Heb. 1. 3. Joh. 1. 14. and *the glory of his people Israel*, Luk. 2. 32. The other priests had garments also for glory and for beauty, v. 40. but they were nothing so glorious and beautiful as Aaron's were; for though as priests they were types of Christ, and so their calling was honorable, and their garments graceful; yet the high priest was therein higher, and therefore in his raiment much more glorious than they, and by this livery of their Lord and master, they were appropriate to his service.

**V. 3.** *{wise hearted}* Skillful artificers.

*{whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom}* The Israelites had now more need to be inspired by God with extraordinary knowledge because (as some observe) they were employed in Egypt rather as drudges, than trained up in an ingenious education, in arts and sciences: and yet had they been brought up in the study of them (wherein there is no proficiency without God's favorable assistance, for all knowledge is from him, even that of the husbandman, Isa. 28. 26.) to such extraordinary curious work, there was need of more than ordinary direction, or instruction. See Chap. 31. 2. & 35. 30.

*{to consecrate him}* That is, to separate him from the rest of the people to God's especial service.

**V. 4.** *{an Ephod}* There was two sorts of ephods, the one made of fine and pure linen, which was common to all priests, 1 Sam. 2. 18. & 22. 18. and used also by other persons upon extraordinary occasion: as David when he danced before the ark, 2 Sam. 6. 14. The other was made of divers materials and colors, peculiar to the high priest, which was a short coat without sleeves, put upon his other garments (to keep them close together) which use is intimated in the Hebrew word; these garments were all of them in number eight, reckoned in divers orders here, and Lev. 8. 7, 8.

**V. 8.** *{curious girdle}* Or, *embroidered*.

**V. 9.** *{Onyx stones}* White stones, like the white of a man's nail, whence they have their name. Anselm. Boetius *Hist. gem.* l. 2. c. 90. Some of them are of divers colors, with white speckles in them. See on v. 20.

**V. 10.** *{the other six}* Of these two sixes, it is like Levi was none among them, for the Levites presented themselves to God, in their daily ministration: and there were twelve besides Levi, because for one Joseph, were reckoned two, Manasseh and Ephraim.

*{according to their birth}* Gen. 43. 33. the eldest first, and the youngest last, see on v. 21.

**V. 11.** *{with the work}* Wisd. 18. 24.

*{in ouches of gold}* That is, hollow places, in which the stones were set fast, as a diamond in a ring.

**V. 12.** *{shall bear their names}* This was a resemblance of Christ our high priest, representing his church to God his father.

*{upon his two shoulders}* The priest was to bear the people on his shoulders, by a patient bearing of their weakness, and waywardness, as well as on his breast, v. 15. by pastoral affection, v. 29.

*{for a memorial}* Not to the people to remember the goodness of the patriarchs, who were many of them more noted for great faults, than for virtues; much less may we think them to be as a help or memory to God, who neither can be ignorant, nor forgetful of anything: But chiefly this memorial was to Aaron to remember the people by intercession for them; and withal to signify that their memory is presented to God, and preserved with God. See Annotations on Gen. 9. 16.

**V. 15.** *{the breastplate of judgment}* Ver. 19. It was so called, because the high priest could not give sentence in judgment without that on his breast; or because when he consulted with God in cases of doubt for a divine judgment, in that case he was to wear it on his breast. See on v. 30. See also 1 Sam. 28. 6. & 30. 7.

**V. 17.** *{a Sardius}* Or, *Rubie*, Exod. 39. 10. a blood-colored stone, and therefore called also a ruby; but Sardius from Sardis, a city of Lydia, in Asia, where such stones are most plentiful; or where there are the rarest, or best of that kind: whereof see Plin. lib. 37. c. 8.

*{a Topaz}* A stone of a golden color, as some say; others say it is green, Plin. lib. 37. Chap. 8. and others, partly of a gray, or sky color: but it seems to be a stone of high price, especially the topaz of Ethiopia, Job 28. 19. the operations of it are many and rare, as Fr. Rueus sets them down, in his second book of gems, and Chap. 5.

*{Carbuncle}* A precious stone shining like lightning, or a glowing coal of fire: the word *Baraketh*, is derived of the word *Baroke*, *to lighten*, Plin. lib. 37. c. 7. col. 253. Rueus *ibidem* c. 18. p. 249. And Anselm. Boetius *de Gem.* l. 2. c. 8.

**V. 18.** *{an Emerald}* A stone of most perfect green color, most pleasant to the eyesight, and profitable, both to preserve it, and to recover it when it is weakened; as Pliny says, lib. 37. c. 5. See Rueus lib. 2. of Gem. c. 4. p. 237.

*{a Sapphire}* Is a precious stone of a light-colored purple, or of a bluish color, like to that which is sometimes seen in the clear sky. Ibid. c. 2. p. 233.

*{a Diamond}* Is a stone bright and white, like crystal, but in a more excellent degree, very hard, Ezek. 1. 9. Zach. 7. 12. Jer. 17. 1. though in those places the Hebrew word be not *Jahalom*, as here, but *Shamir*) so hard, that by blows the hammer and anvil receive more hurt from it, than it from them, Plin. *nat. hist.* lib. 37. c. 10.

**V. 19.** *{a Ligure}* Or, *Lyncure*. A kind of amber, Plin. 37. Chap. 3. a gum hardened into a stone, which stone (in some places) is very precious, and of excellent quality and operation: Rueus lib. 2. c. 18. 26. Some call it a turquoise, or torcois, a stone of a pale green, inclining to sky color, and of strange effects, Ibid. c. 18. p. 256. Some take it for a Hyacinthus, or jacinth, which is of a purple or violet color.

*{an Agate}* Or, *Achate*. A blackish stone, most usually with white streaks upon it, and many of them have much variety of colors, Rueus lib. 2. c. 16. p. 255. which though in some places it be common, is said to be of excellent virtue against many diseases: of this see Plin. lib. 37. c. 10.

*{an Amethyst}* A precious stone, of a pale violet color, Rueus lib. 2. c. 11. p. 244. Of this, see Plin. lib. 37. c. 9.

**V. 20.** *{Beryl}* Is a shining precious stone, whereof there are divers sorts; but the best is of a seawater green color, Plin. lib. 37. Chap. 5.

*{the Onyx stone}* Of that there be many sorts, some say six, some ten, some fourteen, some eighteen; it is hard to say which of them is here meant, Plin. lib. 37. c. 6. See Annot. on v. 9.

*{Jasper}* Of which also there were many sorts of several colors, Rueus lib. 2. of Gem. c. 1. p. 231. See also Plin. lib. 37. v. 8, 9. Many of these stones are mentioned, Rev. 21. 19, 20. and of them many make mystical significations, which wanting ground of Scripture, the building upon them may prove no better than presumptuous fallacies. For though many things of the ceremonial law served to shadow out heavenly things, Heb. 8. 5. yet it is not safe to presume (especially in the explanation of mysteries) *above that which is written*; nor be too punctual in screwing up sensible things to spiritual conformities: as some, both ancient and latter authors have been, especially of the Popish party; nor too bold in bringing down religious matters, to natural allusions, as Josephus does, Antiq. lib. 3. c. 8.

**V. 21.** *{according to the twelve}* It is probable from hence, and from verse 10. that their names were set upon the twelve stones, according to the order of their births; and so Reuben had his name set upon the Sardius, Simeon upon the topaz, and so Benjamin the youngest had his upon the jasper, named last. See the second Annot. on v. 10.

**V. 29.** *{bear the names}* Aaron shall not enter into the holy place, in his own name only, but in the name of all the tribes of Israel. See Annot. on v. 12.

*{breastplate of judgment}* See Annot. on v. 15.

*{upon his heart}* This signifies how near the elect lay to the heart of their high priest Christ Jesus: that is, how heartily he loved them, who parted with his own heart's blood for their sakes: and it is an admonition of the pastor's affectionate respect to his flock; as we see in the apostle's example, 1 Thess. 2. 8.

*{for memorial}* See Annot. on v. 12.

**V. 30.** *{Urim and Thummim}* Signify *light and perfection*, according to the best derivation in the Hebrew tongue; and they are put in the plural number, *lights*, and *perfections*; to note the plenty, as well as the excellency of divine graces. This was meant, as some conceive, of the splendor and perfection of the precious stones, of the breastplate, which were of most clear and perfect beauty: but that is not all; for besides that, these words were engraved, or written upon some of the precious materials of the high priest's ornaments, the *Urim* and *Thummim* was neither made, nor placed by the inspired artificers, but (as some learned writers rather think, by the comparison of Exod. 39. 10. Levit. 8. 8.) made by God himself, and put by Moses into the breastplate. Hence it was called the breastplate of judgment, v. 15. and the judgment given thereby the judgment of *Urim*, Num. 27. 21. because the high priest put it on when he consulted with God, and received from him clear and determinate answers in cases of doubt. These answers were sometimes made by audible voice, sometimes by secret inspiration, which when the priest had told it to the people, the stones and letters in the breastplate, as is probably conceived, (for there is nothing certain of it in the Scripture) might have an extraordinary brightness appearing on them, for assurance that the priest had spoken with the Lord, and received resolution from him; as by the shining of Moses his face, when he came from God, with the tables of the testimony in his hand, Exod. 34. 29, 30. it was manifest that he had from God, what he brought unto the people; and whereas David, desiring to consult with the Lord, called for the ephod, 1 Sam. 23. 9. the reason was, for that the breastplate was fastened to the ephod, v. 28. and the ephod to the breastplate; so that if he had one, he had both, Exod. 39. 21. This oraculous consultation with the breastplate; that is, by the use of the breastplate, continued among the Jews, until about, or somewhat above one hundred years before Christ, as some affirm; but it is generally, and more probably thought, that it lasted no longer, than to the captivity of Babylon: (for which see Ezra 2. 63.) and after that time we find not that the Jews had any use of that kind of revelation. Lev. 8. 8. Ezra 2. 63. Neh. 7. 65.

**V. 32.** *{Habergeon}* A coat of mail, which is a defensive armor, made of iron rings linked one within another, after the manner of network.

**V. 34.** *{A golden Bell and a Pomegranate}* Which were flat and embroidered, and so mixed with the bells, that between two pomegranates, here was placed one bell, and between two bells, one pomegranate. Josephus antiq. l. 3. c. 8. The bell was for sound, and *the priests' lips were to preserve knowledge*, for the instruction of others, Mal. 2. 7. and he must not be mute, Isa. 56. 10. and if he be, it is upon his utmost peril, ver. 33. And it must be a golden bell, for that it is the most precious and purest of metals, such must his doctrine be, Prov. 3. 15. The pomegranates being a sweet and savory fruit, signify his life and conversation, the fruit of his profession, of good relish and acceptation to good people. Some refer the bell to Christ's prophetic office, and the pomegranate, to the sweet savor of the sacrifice of Christ's priestly office, or of his personal perfections, or the graces he gives to his church. See Cant. 4. 3. Chap. 6. 7, 8.

**V. 36.** *{a plate of pure gold}* As some Hebrew doctors say, it was in breadth the measure of two fingers broad, in length, reaching from one ear to another: Josephus thinks it was not a plain plate, but a crown of gold, Antiq. l. 3. c. 8. Which joined to the mitre, might (as some conceive) signify, that Christ, of whom the high priest was a type, was a true Melchisedec, that is, both a king and a priest.
